Zonisamide: (Moderate) Zonisamide use is associated with case reports of decreased sweating, hyperthermia, heat intolerance, or heat stroke and should be used with caution in combination with other drugs that may also predispose patients to heat-related disorders like anticholinergics.
Zuranolone: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of opioids and other CNS depressants, such as zuranolone. Concomitant use can increase the risk of respiratory depression, hypotension, profound sedation, and death. If alternate treatment options are inadequate and coadministration is necessary, limit dosages and durations to the minimum required, monitor patients closely for respiratory depression and sedation, and consider prescribing naloxone for the emergency treatment of opioid overdose.

Adverse Reactions
  Headline
  Adverse Reactions
  General Information
  Opiate agonists present the potential for abuse although the true risk of this phenomenon is extremely low. Physiological dependence, however, will occur during chronic opiate agonist therapy as evidenced by a withdrawal syndrome occurring after abrupt discontinuation of the drug. Symptoms of withdrawal include nausea and diarrhea, coughing, lacrimation, yawning, sneezing, rhinorrhea, profuse sweating, twitching muscles, abdominal and muscles pain/cramps, hot and cold flashes, and piloerection. Elevations in body temperature, respiratory rate, heart rate, and blood pressure may also occur. Without treatment most symptoms resolve within 5—14 days; however, there appears to be a secondary or chronic abstinence syndrome which may last 2—6 months, characterized by insomnia, irritability and muscle aches. The shorter the onset and duration of action of the opiate agonist, the greater the intensity and rapidity of onset of withdrawal symptoms. Routine use of opiate agonists by an expectant mother can lead to depressed respiration in the newborn and a neonatal abstinence syndrome. Withdrawal symptoms in a newborn occur 1—4 days after birth and include generalized tremors, hypertonicity with any form of tactile stimuli, hyperalterness, sleeplessness, excessive crying, vomiting, diarrhea, yawning and fever. It is important to differentiate physiological dependence, the onset of a withdrawal syndrome upon abrupt discontinuation of the drug, and psychological dependence. Psychological dependence is a behavioral syndrome characterized by drug craving, overwhelming concern with acquisition of the drug and other drug-related behaviors such as drug selling and seeking the drug from multiple sources.
 
Pharmacologic tolerance to the analgesic effects of hydrocodone may occur. Tolerance is the need for increasing opiate agonist doses to maintain initial pain relief. Typically, tolerance presents as a decrease in the duration of analgesia and is managed by increasing the opioid dose or frequency. There is no limit to tolerance; thus, some patients may require very large doses of opiate analgesics to control their pain. However, increasing doses of hydrocodone required may be limited by the maximum doses of other agents which are given in combination with hydrocodone. Some patients may require changing to a pure opiate agonist, such as morphine or oxycodone. When increasing doses of analgesia are required, causes may be multi-factorial including tolerance, progression of disease or psychological distress.
 
The most significant adverse effect associated with opiate agonist use is respiratory depression. This results from a decreased sensitivity to carbon dioxide in the brainstem. Respiratory depression is more common in elderly or debilitated patients, following large initial doses in non-opioid tolerant patients or when opioids are given with other agents which cause CNS depression. In addition, bronchopneumonia, coma, cyanosis, death, death neonatal, dyspnea, pulmonary edema, respiratory arrest, and respiratory depression have been reported in children less than 6 years of age in postmarketing reports. Cardio-respiratory arrest has been reported in patients older than 6 years of age during postmarketing surveillance. When hydrocodone is appropriately titrated, the risk of respiratory depression is generally small as tolerance rapidly develops to this effect. Caution needs to be employed in patients with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, decreased pulmonary reserve, cardiovascular disease, or who are taking other CNS depressants. Symptomatic respiratory depression should be treated cautiously with an opioid antagonist such as naloxone. Patients may also develop respiratory depression through sub-acute overdose of an opiate agonist in which sedation builds up slowly leading to a decreased respiratory rate and then respiratory failure. The risk of this is more common with methadone but may be seen with other long-acting opioid preparations or in patients with renal or hepatic impairment. This can be managed by holding 1—2 doses of the opioid and then restarting the opioid at 25% of the previous dose and slowly increasing the dose once the symptoms have resolved. Routine use of opiate agonists by an expectant mother can lead to respiratory depression.
 
Hydrocodone's effect on opiate receptors in the reticular activating system and striatum produces sedation. Patients should be warned that activity requiring mental alertness can be affected because CNS depression, including drowsiness, confusion, and dizziness, can occur. Headache, nervousness, sleep disturbances, dysphoria, euphoria, lightheadedness, alterations of mood (feelings of floating, disorientation, apprehension), anxiety and restlessness may be seen with hydrocodone therapy. Hallucinations (especially of bugs and spiders) and seizures have been reported in patients receiving high doses of opiate agonists. Depending upon the individual patients tolerance, hallucinations may be reported in patients undergoing rapid dose escalation.
 
Patients may complain of adverse GI effects while taking hydrocodone. Opiate analgesics can cause a variety of effects, most commonly nausea/vomiting and constipation. Nausea and vomiting is most commonly seen at the initiation of therapy or when switching agents. Opiate agonists affect the vestibular system and may cause more nausea/vomiting in ambulatory patients than bedridden patients. Scheduled treatment with an antiemetic during the first 1—2 days, then as needed during opiate therapy will usually control these symptoms until tolerance develops. If patients have nausea associated with movement, an antivertigo agent such as meclizine may be appropriate. Metoclopramide may be helpful in treating the symptoms of early satiety or fullness. Constipation due to decrease GI motility and secretions is common during opiate agonist therapy. Tolerance rarely develops to this effect; therefore, patients require a bowel regimen consisting of a stool softener and mild stimulant throughout opioid therapy. If the patient does not have a bowel movement for 3 days, an enema or suppository should be administered to prevent impaction.
 
Opiate analgesics also can cause spasm of the sphincter of Oddi, with morphine producing greater effects than meperidine and meperidine producing greater effects than codeine. The incidence with hydrocodone is not known. Hyperamylasemia, secondary to drug-induced spasm of the sphincter of Oddi, has been associated with various opiate agonists. While serum amylase and lipase concentrations can rise as a result of biliary obstruction or spasm, overt pancreatitis is rare with opiate analgesics. Gastritis and hepatotoxicity are also relatively rare with these agents.
 
Opiate agonists can exert adverse effects on the endocrine system. Libido decrease may occur following a decrease in production of luteinizing hormone and a corresponding decrease in testosterone. Women may experience amenorrhea and infertility and men may be unable to attain or maintain an erection. Opiate agonists can inhibit the release of thyrotropin, leading to a decrease in thyroid hormone. Hydrocodone and related compounds can stimulate the release of vasopressin (ADH). Hyponatremia can occur as a result of SIADH.
 
Hydrocodone and other opiate agonists are known to cause pruritus. In the absence of rash or other symptoms, the pruritus is probably not related to an allergic reaction. Opiate agonist-induced pruritus is thought to be mediated through stimulation of central opiate receptors since opiate antagonists (i.e., nalmefene or naloxone) have been shown to ameliorate pruritus associated with cholestasis. Use of H1-blockers or changing to a different opioid may lessen the pruritus.
 
Hydrocodone and related compounds can cause cardiovascular adverse reactions due to anticholinergic effects and their ability to release histamine. These reactions include sinus bradycardia, sinus tachycardia, palpitations, hypertension, hypotension, orthostatic hypotension, syncope, and flushing. Children over 6 years of age have experienced concomitant respiratory and cardiac arrest.
 
Anticholinergic effects occur infrequently with hydrocodone. Patients can experience xerostomia (dry mouth), blurred vision, or urinary retention.
 
Hydrocodone, like morphine, can cause miosis. Therapeutic doses can increase accommodation and sensitivity to light reflex and decrease intraocular tension in both normal and glaucomatous eyes. Severe overdose can cause mydriasis once brain anoxia develops.
 
Accidental overdose and death have been reported in children less than 6 years of age during postmarketing surveillance (see respiratory arrest); accidental and non-accidental overdose and death due to drug toxicity have been reported in patients older than 6 years of age (see cardiac arrest).
  Adverse Reactions
    Headline
    agitation, anxiety, ataxia, confusion, depression, dizziness, drowsiness, dyskinesia, dysphoria, emotional lability, euphoria, fatigue, hallucinations, headache, impaired cognition, insomnia, lethargy, migraine, psychomotor impairment, restlessness, seizures, tinnitus, tremor, vertigo
    General Information
    Hydrocodone is a central nervous system (CNS) depressant. The effect of hydrocodone on opiate receptors in the reticular activating system and striatum produces sedation. Homatropine is an anticholinergic agent used in sub-therapeutic amounts; however, some patients may be more sensitive to the effects of this drug, even at lower doses. The most common CNS adverse reactions to homatropine; hydrocodone include drowsiness (sedation, somnolence, mental clouding, lethargy), decreased mental alertness, impaired cognition, psychomotor impairment, lightheadedness, dizziness, and headache. Patients should be warned that activity requiring mental alertness may be impaired because impaired mental and physical performance can occur. The following adverse reactions have been identified during clinical studies, in the literature, or during post-approval use of hydrocodone and/or homatropine. Psychiatric adverse events have included agitation, anxiety, confusion, fear, dysphoria, depression, hallucinations, euphoria, alterations of mood (feelings of floating, disorientation, apprehension, emotional lability), and restlessness. Hallucinations have been reported in patients receiving high doses or accelerated dosage titrations of opiate agonists. Other reported CNS-related reactions with hydrocodone and/or homatropine have included ataxia, facial dyskinesia, insomnia, migraine, seizures, tremor, tinnitus, and vertigo. Related general reactions may include fatigue and falls/injury.

    Headline
    bronchial secretions, coma, cough, cyanosis, dyspnea, increased intracranial pressure, nasal congestion, nasal dryness, pharyngitis, pulmonary edema, respiratory arrest, respiratory depression, sinusitis, wheezing
    General Information
    The most significant adverse effect associated with homatropine; hydrocodone use, as with other opiate agonists, is respiratory depression. This results from a decreased sensitivity to carbon dioxide (CO2) in the brainstem. Reduced respiratory drive, and the resultant CO2 retention can cause increased intracranial pressure, particularly in patients at risk. Overdose in adult and pediatric patients 6 years of age and older has also been associated with fatal respiratory depression. Postmarketing events seen in patients older than 6 years of age include accidental overdose, cardio-respiratory arrest, coma, death due to drug toxicity, non-accidental overdose, and excessive dose. In children less than 6 years, opioid-containing drug products have been associated with fatal respiratory depression; postmarketing events seen in children less than 6 years of age include accidental overdose, bronchopneumonia, coma, cyanosis, death, dyspnea, pulmonary edema, respiratory arrest, and respiratory depression. The following adverse reactions have been identified during clinical studies, in the literature, or during post-approval use of hydrocodone and/or homatropine; the incidences are not known. Other reported respiratory reactions include bronchitis, dyspnea, nasal congestion, naso-pharyngitis, sinusitis, upper respiratory tract infection, thickening of bronchial secretions, tightness of the chest and wheezing, dry nose (nasal dryness), dry throat, and worsening cough. Since dyspnea and wheezing can also indicate worsening of a medical condition, excessive dosage, or hypersensitivity, patients with these symptoms should seek medical attention immediately.

    General Information
    Flushing and hyperhidrosis are dermatologic reactions that may occur with homatropine; hydrocodone. Pruritus, urticaria, and rash (unspecified) may also occur as dermatologic or allergic effects. Serious hypersensitivity reactions, including anaphylaxis and anaphylactoid reactions, have occurred to opiate agonists, including hydrocodone. If urticaria, dyspnea or wheezing, or other symptoms of a serious allergic reaction are present, the patient should seek medical attention. If pruritus occurs in the absence of a rash, it may not be related to an allergic reaction. Opiate agonist-induced pruritus is thought to be mediated through stimulation of central opiate receptors. Use of antihistamines or changing to a different opioid may lessen the pruritus.

    Headline
    abdominal pain, biliary obstruction, constipation, dyspepsia, dysphagia, gastroesophageal reflux, GI obstruction, hyperamylasemia, ileus, nausea, opioid-induced esophageal dysfunction, pancreatitis, vomiting, xerostomia
    General Information
    Gastrointestinal (GI) complaints are fairly common with the use of homatropine; hydrocodone antitussive preparations; dry mouth (xerostomia), nausea, vomiting, and constipation are the most commonly reported GI side effects. Nausea and vomiting are most commonly seen at the initiation of therapy or when switching agents. Induction of nausea and vomiting possibly occurs from direct stimulation of the vestibular system and/or the chemoreceptor trigger zone. Constipation may occur with continued administration of hydrocodone and is due to decreased GI motility and secretions. Patients rarely become tolerant to this effect; therefore, advise patients to follow a bowel regimen consistently during opioid therapy. Increased fluid and fiber intake and the use of stool softeners and laxatives are recommended. Treat persistent symptoms of constipation as required to prevent impaction. The following adverse reactions have been identified during clinical studies, in the literature, or during post-approval use of hydrocodone and/or homatropine; their incidence cannot be accurately estimated. Abdominal pain, bowel GI obstruction, decreased appetite, diarrhea, dysphagia, gastroesophageal reflux (GERD), indigestion or dyspepsia, pancreatitis, paralytic ileus, and biliary tract spasm (spasm of the sphincter of Oddi). Hyperamylasemia, secondary to drug-induced spasm of the sphincter of Oddi, has been associated with various opiate agonists. While serum amylase and lipase concentrations can rise as a result of biliary obstruction or spasm, overt pancreatitis is rare with opiate use. Cases of opioid-induced esophageal dysfunction (OIED) have been reported in individuals taking opioids. The risk of OIED may increase as the dose and/or duration of opioids increases. Regularly evaluate individuals for signs and symptoms of OIED (e.g., dysphagia, regurgitation, non-cardiac chest pain), and, if necessary, adjust opioid therapy as clinically appropriate.

    General Information
    The following cardiovascular adverse reactions have been identified during clinical studies, in the literature, or during post-approval use of hydrocodone and/or homatropine; their incidence cannot be reliably estimated. Cardiovascular effects reported have included peripheral edema, increased blood pressure, decreased blood pressure, sinus tachycardia, chest pain (unspecified), palpitations, syncope, orthostatic hypotension, QT prolongation, and hot flushes (peripheral vasodilation or hot flashes). Severe hypotension, including orthostatic hypotension and syncope, may occur in ambulatory patients, particularly with excessive dosage of opioid medications. There is increased risk in patients whose ability to maintain blood pressure has already been compromised by a reduced blood volume or concurrent administration of certain CNS depressant drugs. Vasodilation that occurs in patients experiencing circulatory shock can further reduce cardiac output and blood pressure.

    General Information
    Both homatropine and hydrocodone may cause effects on the urinary tract that may lead to urinary retention. Anticholinergic effects may occur with homatropine, even though the amount of homatropine in these antitussive products is small and simply provided to deter abuse of the opioid.

    Headline
    blurred vision, diplopia, miosis, xerophthalmia
    General Information
    Blurred vision, diplopia, miosis (constricted pupils), and visual disturbances have been reported with the use of hydrocodone-containing cough preparations. Hydrocodone, like morphine, can cause miosis at usual therapeutic doses. Therapeutic doses of homatropine; hydrocodone can increase accommodation and sensitivity to light reflex and decrease intraocular tension in both normal and glaucomatous eyes. Severe opioid overdose can cause mydriasis once brain anoxia develops. Homatropine, though present in lower than therapeutic amounts in homatropine; hydrocodone antitussive products, can increase intraocular pressure due to the anticholinergic actions. Elderly patients are more susceptible to these effects, including possible precipitation of undiagnosed glaucoma. Other ocular effects resulting from the anticholinergic effects of homatropine include blurred vision and xerophthalmia.

    Headline
    adrenocortical insufficiency, amenorrhea, impotence (erectile dysfunction), infertility, libido decrease
    General Information
    Opiate agonists, such as hydrocodone, can exert adverse effects on the hormonal and endocrine systems. Cases of adrenocortical insufficiency have been reported with opioid use, more often following greater than 1 month of use. Presentation of adrenal insufficiency may include non-specific symptoms and signs including nausea, vomiting, anorexia, fatigue, weakness, dizziness, and low blood pressure. If adrenal insufficiency is suspected, confirm the diagnosis with diagnostic testing as soon as possible. If adrenal insufficiency is diagnosed, treat with physiologic replacement doses of corticosteroids. Wean the patient off of the opioid to allow adrenal function to recover and continue corticosteroid treatment until adrenal function recovers. Other opioids may be tried as some cases reported use of a different opioid without recurrence of adrenal insufficiency. The information available does not identify any particular opioids as being more likely to be associated with adrenal insufficiency. Hypogonadism and infertility have been rarely reported with the use of hydrocodone-containing products. Cases of androgen deficiency have also occurred with chronic use of opioids. Chronic use of opioids may influence the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, leading to androgen deficiency that may manifest as symptoms as low libido (libido decrease), impotence (erectile dysfunction), amenorrhea, or infertility. The causal role of opioids in the syndrome of hypogonadism is unknown because the various medical, physical, lifestyle, and psychological stressors that may influence gonadal hormone levels have not been adequately controlled for in studies conducted to date.

    Headline
    neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome, neonatal respiratory depression, physiological dependence, psychological dependence, withdrawal
    General Information
    As with other opiate agonists, the use of hydrocodone presents the potential for abuse. Although the true prevalence of addiction to opioids in chronic pain patients is unknown, estimates from multiple studies indicate that it may range from 0% to 50% and 0% to 7.7% in chronic non-malignant pain and cancer pain, respectively. Psychological dependence, however, will occur during chronic homatropine; hydrocodone therapy as evidenced by a withdrawal syndrome occurring after abrupt discontinuation of the drug. Homatropine; hydrocodone is indicated for short-term use; therefore, addiction and physiological dependence are not expected to occur. Symptoms of withdrawal include nausea and diarrhea, coughing, lacrimation, yawning, sneezing, rhinorrhea, profuse sweating, twitching muscles, abdominal and muscle pain/cramps, hot and cold flashes, and piloerection. Elevations in body temperature, respiratory rate, heart rate, and blood pressure may also occur. Without treatment, most symptoms resolve within 5 to 14 days; however, there appears to be a secondary or chronic abstinence syndrome which may last 2 to 6 months, characterized by insomnia, irritability and muscle aches. The shorter the onset and duration of action of the opiate agonist, the greater the intensity and rapidity of onset of withdrawal symptoms. Neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome (NOWS) and neonatal respiratory depression may occur in neonates and infants exposed to opioids in utero for an extended period of time. Neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome may present as irritability, hyperactivity, abnormal sleep pattern, high-pitched cry, tremor, vomiting, diarrhea, and failure to gain weight. It can be life-threatening if not recognized and treated. Severe symptoms may require pharmacologic therapy managed by clinicians familiar with neonatal opioid withdrawal. Onset, duration, and severity of opioid withdrawal may vary based on the specific opioid used, duration of use, timing and amount of last maternal use, and rate of elimination by the newborn.

    Tolerance to homatropine; hydrocodone is not usually a concern, given the short-term use in most patients, and the labeled indication for temporary treatment of cough. Pharmacologic tolerance to the analgesic effects of hydrocodone may occur with continued use. Tolerance is the need for increasing opiate agonist doses to maintain therapeutic effect.

Description
  Text
  Homatropine and hydrocodone are used together in an oral preparation as an antitussive for cough relief. Hydrocodone is a semisynthetic opiate agonist antitussive and is similar in activity to other phenanthrene derivatives such as codeine. Homatropine, an anticholinergic agent, is included in subtherapeutic amounts in hydrocodone antitussive products to discourage abuse and deliberate overdosage. Opioid cough and cold medicines that contain hydrocodone are not indicated for pediatric patients under 18 years of age. The products are contraindicated in neonates, infants and children less than 6 years of age; the risks of these medicines outweigh their benefits in patients younger than 18 years.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Homatropine; hydrocodone is used as an antitussive for the treatment of cough. Recommended doses of hydrocodone for the treatment of cough are typically lower than those used in the treatment of pain.
Hydrocodone: Hydrocodone acts as a weak agonist at opiate receptors within the central nervous system (CNS). Opiate receptors include delta, kappa, and mu. Hydrocodone primarily affects mu opioid receptors. The metabolite hydromorphone is also a mu opiate receptor agonist. TThe relative contributions of the parent drug and the metabolite hydromorphone to the antitussive and analgesic effects are unknown. The antitussive effects of opiate agonists are mediated through direct action on receptors in the cough center of the medulla. Cough suppression can be achieved at lower doses than those required to produce analgesia. Hydrocodone also has a drying effect on the respiratory track and increases the viscosity of respiratory secretions.
Homatropine: Homatropine is added to deter abuse. Although not present in therapeutic amounts, homatropine inhibits muscarinic actions of acetylcholine on smooth muscle and structures affected by postganglionic cholinergic nerves. Postganglionic receptors are located in smooth muscle, cardiac muscle, sinoatrial and atrioventricular nodes, and exocrine glands. Most effects are dose-dependent.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Hydrocodone: Hydrocodone is administered orally and is well absorbed from the GI tract. Following a 10 mg oral dose, the peak concentration was 23.6 ng/mL, with the time to peak concentration (Tmax) achieved at 1.3 hours. Antitussive activity lasts for 4 to 6 hours.
Homatropine (present in subtherapeutic amounts): Oral absorption is poor and irregular. Total absorption is about 10% to 25%.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    The pharmacokinetics of homatropine; hydrocodone products have not been characterized in patients with hepatic impairment; however, patients with severe hepatic impairment may have higher hydrocodone plasma concentrations than those with normal hepatic function and may have increased or prolonged effects. Such patients may need dosage adjustments and should be monitored closely for opioid toxicity, including sedation, respiratory depression and hypotension.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Hydrocodone and its metabolites are eliminated primarily in the kidneys. Patients with renal impairment may have higher plasma concentrations of hydrocodone than those with normal function. These patients should be monitored closely for hydrocodone toxicity; some patients may need lower or less frequent dosing.
    Name
    Other
    Description
    CYPD26 Poor Metabolizers (CYP2D6 PMs)
In CYP2D6 poor metabolizers, hydrocodone O-demethylation to hydromorphone is impaired. Patients who are CYP2D6 poor metabolizers may have decreased therapeutic effects from hydrocodone. About 7% to 10% of the white population lack the functional enzyme by which O-demethylation to hydromorphone occurs.
  Text
  Hydrocodone; homatropine products are administered orally.
Hydrocodone: Hydrocodone undergoes complex hepatic metabolism via O-demethylation, N-demethylation, and 6-keto reduction to the corresponding 6-alpha- and 6-beta-hydroxy active metabolites. The O-demethylation of hydrocodone is predominantly catalyzed by CYP2D6, which yields hydromorphone. Hydromorphone may contribute to therapeutic effect. Patients who are receiving agents that may inhibit CYP2D6 or who are CYP2D6 poor metabolizers may have increased or decreased analgesic or therapeutic effects from hydrocodone. The N-demethylation of hydrocodone appears to be catalyzed by CYP3A4, which yields norhydrocodone, an inactive metabolite. About 40% of hydrocodone metabolism appears to be through non-CYP pathways. The elimination half-life of hydrocodone is about 4 hours.
Homatropine (present in subtherapeutic amounts): Homatropine is thought to undergo hepatic metabolism by enzymatic hydrolysis and is excreted through the urine and feces.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 (CYP450) isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP2D6, CYP3A4
Hydrocodone is metabolized to norhydrocodone via CYP3A4 and to hydromorphone via CYP2D6. The formation of these metabolites may theoretically be affected by drugs that inhibit/induce these enzymes; therefore, coadministration of homatropine; hydrocodone with CYP3A4 or CYP2D6 inhibitors or CYP3A4 inducers should be avoided.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see the specific product information within the How Supplied section.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Administration Details
      Name
      Oral Liquid Formulations
      Description
      Advise patients/caregivers to use a calibrated spoon or other measuring device. Do not to overfill.
Rinse the measuring device with water after each use.
    Name
    Oral Administration
    Description
    Administer orally with a full glass of water. May be taken with food or milk to minimize GI irritation.
Cough preparations should not be administered more frequently than every 4 hours; do not administer more frequently than recommended or prescribed as severe, possibly fatal, respiratory depression may occur.
An unresponsive cough should be reevaluated in 5 days or sooner for possible underlying pathology, such as foreign body or lower respiratory tract disease.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Specific dose adjustment recommendations are not available; decreased elimination of both drugs can occur. Use with caution in patients with severe hepatic impairment, and monitor closely for signs of hydrocodone toxicity (respiratory depression, sedation, and hypotension). Based on recommendations for some analgesic products containing hydrocodone, consider an initial reduction in hydrocodone dosage of 50% for patients with hepatic impairment.
  Renal Impairment
  Specific dose adjustment recommendations are not available; however, deceased elimination of both drugs can occur. Use with caution in patients with moderate to severe renal impairment, and monitor closely for signs of hydrocodone toxicity (respiratory depression, sedation, and hypotension). Based on recommendations for some analgesic products containing hydrocodone, consider an initial reduction in hydrocodone dosage of 50% for patients with moderate to severe renal impairment.
